Simple kernel without tensor cores: ```cuda __global__ void gemm_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, // MxK const int8_t* __restrict__ B, // KxN (int8) const half* __restrict__ scales, // num_groups x N half* __restrict__ C, // MxN int M, int N, int K, int group_size, int num_groups) { int n0 = blockIdx.x * block_n; // block_n e.g., 128 int n_size = min(block_n, N - n0); // Each thread computes a sub-tile of output (m, n) // Use 2D thread block: threads_m x threads_n int tm = threadIdx.y; // e.g., 4 int tn = threadIdx.x; // e.g., 32 half accum[tm_count][tn_count]; // small for (int k_base = 0; k_base < K; k_base += tile_k) { // load A tile (M x tile_k) into shared // load B tile (tile_k x n_size) into shared __syncthreads(); int group_id = k_base / group_size; #pragma unroll for (int kk = 0; kk < tile_k; ++kk) { int k = k_base + kk; half a_val = A_s[thread_m][kk]; // from shmem // load B column for this n int8_t b_int = B_s[kk][thread_n]; half scale = scales_s[group_id][thread_n]; // scale for this group and n half b_val = __hmul(__int2half_rn((int)b_int), scale); accum += a_val * b_val; } __syncthreads(); } C[out_m * N + out_n] = accum; } ``` Wait, scales are per group per column. So for each K position, we need the scale for the corresponding group and column. If we load scales into shared memory or compute group_id on the fly, we can apply them. But note: for symmetric quantization, `dequant = q * scale`. The scale is `max_abs / 7.0`. So the original float weight is approximately `q * scale`. The output of GEMM with dequantized weights should match `A @ (q * scale)`. So the kernel can be: compute `sum_k A[m,k] * (int8(k,n) * scale[group(k), n])`. This is equivalent to `(A @ int8) * scale` if scales are column-wise constant? No, scales vary per group and per column, but within a group, all K rows share the same scale for a given column. Now, let's think about the actual performance on H100. We want to write a kernel that is fast. Since M=16 is small, we can use one warp group or multiple warps per output tile. A simple but potentially fast approach: - Each block processes a tile of M=16 rows and `TN` columns. - Block dimension: e.g., 128 threads (4 warps). - TN = 128. - Tile K = 64 or 128. - Use shared memory for A tile: 16 x TK x 2 bytes. For TK=128, that's 4KB. - Use shared memory for B tile: TK x TN x 1 byte (int8). For TK=128, TN=128, that's 16KB. - Use shared memory for scales: (TK/group_size) x TN x 2 bytes. For TK=128, group_size=128, that's 1 x 128 x 2 = 256 bytes. - Total per stage ~20KB. We can double buffer, so 40KB. Very small. - Launch enough blocks to saturate SMs: N / TN = 4096 / 128 = 32 blocks. That's only 32 blocks. H100 has ~132 SMs. 32 blocks is too few to fully utilize the GPU. We need to split N further or split K. We can have each block process multiple N tiles. For example, have 128 blocks, each processing TN=32 columns. Or even TN=16, 256 blocks. Or we can have blocks process a K partition and then reduce. But reduction adds overhead. Alternatively, process M dimension too: M=16. We can have each block handle only a subset of rows (e.g., 4 rows) and all N columns? But N=4096. That would be 4 blocks for M, each reading a large portion of B. Better: use a 2D grid where blockIdx.x covers N (e.g., 128 chunks of 32 = 4096 => 128 blocks) and blockIdx.y covers M (e.g., 2 chunks of 8 => 2 blocks). Total blocks = 256. That might saturate the SMs better. But since M=16, splitting M into 2 chunks of 8 means each block handles 8 rows. A tile of A is 8 x TK. For TK=128, that's 2KB. B tile is 128 x 32 = 4KB. Total ~6KB per stage. With 256 blocks, each SM gets ~2 blocks. On H100, each SM can run many warps. This might be okay. But the workload per block is small: 8 * 32 * 4096 = ~1M MACs. At 1 TFLOPS per SM, that's 1 microsecond. But memory bound means it's limited by bandwidth. H100 has 3.35 TB/s = 3350 GB/s. Each block reads 8*4096*2 + 4096*32*1 + scales = 64KB + 128KB + small = 192KB. 256 blocks * 192KB = 49MB total reads. At 3350 GB/s, that's ~15 microseconds. This is a rough estimate. Wait, total memory read for the whole matmul if we read B once and A M times: A is 128KB, B is 8MB. Total 8.1MB. At 3350 GB/s, that's ~2.4 microseconds. So if we do it in 256 blocks, each block reads a part of B, but collectively B is read once. Overhead is small. So 256 blocks of 128 threads each = 32768 threads. That's a lot of threads, which helps hide latency. Let's design the kernel more concretely: ```cuda __global__ void marlin_gemm_custom( const half* __restrict__ A, // M x K const int8_t* __restrict__ B, // K x N const half* __restrict__ scales, // num_groups x N half* __restrict__ C, // M x N int M, int N, int K, int group_size, int num_groups) { const int TN = 32; // columns per block const int TK = 128; // K tile size const int TM = 16; // but M is small, we handle all M? Or split M across block.y int n0 = blockIdx.x * TN; int m0 = blockIdx.y * TM; // if we set TM=8, blockDim.y=1 // Let's handle M per warp to avoid splitting M. } ``` Actually, since M=16, it's better to have each block handle all M=16 rows for a chunk of N, using all threads to parallelize over K and N. For example, block size = 128 threads. Each thread computes a single output element (m, n) or a small tile. If each thread computes one output element, we need 16 * TN threads. If TN=64, that's 1024 threads, which is 8 warps, too many for a block if we want many blocks. If TN=32, that's 512 threads (4 warps). N=4096 => 128 blocks. Total threads = 65536. This is good. But each thread would need to iterate over K=4096 and do 4096 FMAs. That's a lot of instructions per thread (4096). At 1.5 GHz, a thread can do ~1e3 ops per microsecond, so 4096 ops might take ~4 microseconds, plus memory latency. This might be okay if we have many warps. However, the memory access pattern for B would be: each thread loads K int8 values for its fixed n. That's a strided access across K, not coalesced. To coalesce, threads in a warp should load adjacent elements. So we should parallelize K across threads in a warp. So loading B from a row-major layout is inherently inefficient for small N_chunk. This is exactly why the Marlin format interleaves columns and rows into tiles: so that a tile of 16x16 is stored contiguously and can be loaded with a few vectorized loads. If we store our weights in a row-major int8 format, the kernel's global memory loads for B will be strided and inefficient. However, we can transpose the weight matrix to column-major! If B is column-major (N, K), then for a block handling N_chunk columns, the K dimension is contiguous! Specifically, B_colmajor[n][k]. Then `B[n0 + c][k0 + kk]` is contiguous in kk for fixed c. But we can load each column via a vectorized load. So storing B in column-major (or N-leading) format makes loads for small N_chunk much better. Let's define our weight storage as column-major: `int8 B_col[N][K]`. Then for a block with n0, the slice is `B_col[n0..n0+7][k0..k0+127]`. We can load this by having each thread handle a portion of a column. For 128 threads and 8 columns * 128 bytes = 1024 bytes, each thread loads 8 bytes. Each column is 128 bytes. 16 threads per column load 8 bytes each. So thread `c*16 + sub` loads `B_col[n0 + c][k0 + sub*8]` for 8 bytes (1 int64). This is perfectly coalesced within each group of 16 threads! The 16 threads accessing the same column read contiguous 128 bytes. That's a single 128-byte cache line. Great! Then we can store into B_s either in the same layout or transposed. For the compute phase, we need `B_s[kk][c]`. If we store B_s as row-major, we need to transpose during the load, which is easy: each thread reads 8 bytes and writes them to B_s[r..r+7][c] (i.e., along kk dimension). But writing to shared memory with strided indices may have bank conflicts. Alternatively, we can store B_s in column-major: `B_s[c][kk]`. Then during compute, each thread reads `B_s[c][kk]` for its column c. This is a strided access across threads: threads in a warp have different c, same kk. If B_s is column-major, then `B_s[c][kk]` accesses different rows in shared memory. Since each column is 128 bytes (for BK=128), column c starts at offset c*128. Thread with c reads offset c*128 + kk. Since columns are spaced by 128 bytes = 32 banks, consecutive c map to the same bank offset? Actually bank = (address / 4) % 32. Address of col c = c*128 + kk. Bank = (c*128 + kk)/4 % 32 = (c*32 + kk/4) % 32. For c=0..7, bank = kk/4, kk/4+32, etc. Mod 32, c=0 and c=1 differ by 32 -> same bank. c=0 and c=1 map to same bank! Because 128 bytes is exactly 32 banks * 4 bytes. So spacing by 128 bytes causes all columns to alias the same banks. This leads to 8-way bank conflicts if threads with c=0..7 read simultaneously. To avoid this, we need to pad the column stride in shared memory to something not a multiple of 128 bytes, e.g., 132 bytes. But we can also just store B_s in row-major and tolerate the 4-way bank conflict we analyzed earlier. That might be simpler. Given the memory-bound nature, bank conflicts in shared memory are not the bottleneck. Let's proceed with row-major shared memory for B_s. But we need to load from global memory efficiently. So store weights in column-major global memory! We can do the transpose during packing in Python. That's easy. Let's outline the weight setup in Python: ```python # w shape (K, N) w = torch.randn(K, N, dtype=torch.float16, device='cuda') # groupwise quantize w_reshaped = w.view(num_groups, group_size, N) max_abs = w_reshaped.abs().amax(dim=1, keepdim=True) scales = (max_abs / 7.0).squeeze(1) # (num_groups, N) q = torch.round(w_reshaped / max_abs * 7.0).to(torch.int8) # Actually: q = torch.round(w_reshaped / scales.unsqueeze(1)).to(torch.int8) q = torch.clamp(q, -8, 7) q = q.reshape(K, N) # Convert q to column-major for our kernel (N, K) # But our kernel needs int8 B of shape (N, K) (column-major access) # In CUDA, we can just pass q_t = q.t().contiguous() which is (N, K) row-major in PyTorch, # but in CUDA we treat it as column-major for the original (K,N) matrix. b_q_weight = q.t().contiguous() # shape (N, K), dtype int8 scales = scales.contiguous() # (num_groups, N) ``` Wait, if we transpose q to (N, K) and store row-major, then element `b_q_weight[n, k]` corresponds to original `q[k, n]`. In our kernel, we want to load `B[n0 + c][k0 + kk]`. If we treat the pointer as `int8* B` and index `B[(n0 + c) * K + (k0 + kk)]`, that's reading `q_t` row-major, which corresponds to `q[k0+kk, n0+c]`. Perfect. Now, the kernel: ```cuda __global__ void gemm_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const int8_t* __restrict__ B, // column-major: shape (N, K) in our indexing const half* __restrict__ scales, half* __restrict__ C, int M, int N, int K, int group_size) { const int BN = 8; const int BK = 128; int n0 = blockIdx.x * BN; int n_base = n0; // Shared memory __shared__ half A_s[16][BK]; // M=16 __shared__ int8_t B_s[BK][BN]; __shared__ half S_s[1][BN]; // since BK <= group_size (128) half accum[16]; // actually we need M accumulators per thread // Wait, each thread handles one column and multiple rows? Instead of column-major (n*K + k), we can store B in blocks: `int8 B_blocked[N/BN][K/BK][BN][BK]`. That is, blocked layout. This is similar to what Marlin does but simpler. Let's do that! Since we control the weight packing in Python, we can choose any layout. Blocked layout for BN=8, BK=128: - The tensor is divided into tiles of 128 (K) x 8 (N). - There are N/8 = 512 tiles along N, K/128 = 32 tiles along K. - We store them as `(num_n_tiles, num_k_tiles, BN, BK)`. - In PyTorch: `q_blocked = q.reshape(K//BK, BK, N//BN, BN).permute(2, 0, 3, 1).contiguous()`. - q shape: (K, N) - Reshape to (num_k_tiles, BK, num_n_tiles, BN) - Permute to (num_n_tiles, num_k_tiles, BN, BK) - Flatten or keep as 4D. - Then each block tile `t_n, t_k` is contiguous in memory: `offset = ((t_n * num_k_tiles + t_k) * BN + b_n) * BK + b_k`. - In the kernel, for block `blockIdx.x`, it corresponds to n-tile index `t_n = blockIdx.x`. Because each block handles BN=8 columns. So `t_n = blockIdx.x` (assuming grid is 1D and covers all N/BN tiles). - Wait, N=4096, BN=8 => 512 blocks. Yes. - For each k-tile `t` in 0..31, the block loads tile `t_n, t`. - Address of start of tile: `B + ((t_n * num_k_tiles + t) * BN * BK)`. - Inside the tile, layout is `(BN, BK)`. So `B_s[b_n][b_k]` maps to `tile_base + b_n * BK + b_k`. - This is perfect! The tile is `BN * BK = 1024` bytes contiguous. Loading this tile: ```cuda const int8_t* B_tile = B + ((blockIdx.x * num_k_tiles + t) * BN * BK); for (int i = tid; i < BN * BK; i += blockDim.x) { B_s[i / BN][i % BN] = B_tile[i]; // Wait, B_s shape is [BK][BN]. Need to map i to (b_k, b_n). } ``` Actually, if the blocked layout storage is `(BN, BK)`, then `i = b_n * BK + b_k`. So `b_n = i / BK`, `b_k = i % BK`. We want B_s[k][n] = B_tile[b_n * BK + b_k]. So: ```cuda for (int i = tid; i < BN * BK; i += blockDim.x) { int b_n = i / BK; int b_k = i % BK; B_s[b_k][b_n] = B_tile[i]; } ``` This loop has consecutive threads reading consecutive `i`, so loads are coalesced. Let's write the packing in Python: ```python class ModelNew(nn.Module): def __init__(self, k, n, group_size): super().__init__() self.k = k self.n = n self.group_size = group_size num_groups = k // group_size # generate weights w = torch.randn(k, n, dtype=torch.float16, device='cuda') # quantize symmetric signed 4-bit w_reshaped = w.view(num_groups, group_size, n) max_abs = w_reshaped.abs().amax(dim=1, keepdim=True) self.scales = (max_abs / 7.0).squeeze(1).contiguous() # (num_groups, n) q = torch.round(w_reshaped / self.scales.unsqueeze(1)).to(torch.int8) q = torch.clamp(q, -8, 7) q = q.reshape(k, n) # Pack to 4-bit: 8 nibbles per uint32, along columns (n) # We want shape (n//8, k) where each element is uint32 of 8 weights. # For each n_group (8 columns), the values are q[:, n_group*8 + c]. q_uint8 = q.to(torch.uint8) # Convert signed to nibble (two's complement) # Python: negative values are represented as large unsigned ints, but bitwise & 0xF gives low 4 bits. nibble = (q_uint8 & 0xF) # Pack 8 nibbles into uint32 nibble = nibble.view(k, n // 8, 8) word = ( nibble[..., 0].to(torch.uint32) | (nibble[..., 1].to(torch.uint32) << 4) | (nibble[..., 2].to(torch.uint32) << 8) | (nibble[..., 3].to(torch.uint32) << 12) | (nibble[..., 4].to(torch.uint32) << 16) | (nibble[..., 5].to(torch.uint32) << 20) | (nibble[..., 6].to(torch.uint32) << 24) | (nibble[..., 7].to(torch.uint32) << 28) ) # word shape: (k, n//8) self.b_q_weight = word.t().contiguous() # (n//8, k) self.register_buffer('b_q_weight', self.b_q_weight) self.register_buffer('scales', self.scales) # No workspace needed. ``` Wait, we used `self.scales = ...` and `self.b_q_weight = ...` before `register_buffer`. We should assign to buffers directly. Let's refine: ```python self.register_buffer('scales', self.scales) ``` Actually, the tensor should be created on cuda. One more thing: the reference may compute scales in a different way. For example, some implementations compute `scale = max_abs / 8.0` and quantize to [-8, 7]. But the dequantization uses `scale`. In that case, the maximum quantized value would be 7 * scale = max_abs * 7/8. That clips the top 1/8 of the range. Most implementations use `max_abs / 7.0` to avoid clipping, but might clamp after rounding. Let's check: if we use `max_abs / 7.0`, then `q = round(w / scale)` might exceed 7 for some values. We clamp to 7. The max abs weight maps to 7 (or -8 if it's negative and max_abs is positive? Actually if max_abs is positive, the most negative weight might be -max_abs, which rounds to -7 (since -max_abs/scale = -7). But the range is [-8,7], so -8 is not used if weights are symmetric and max_abs is positive. That's fine. Some implementations compute `scale = max_abs / 7.0` but also handle negative max. Since `abs()` is used, it's fine. I think `scale = max_abs / 7.0` is standard. Now, for the unpacking in CUDA: we need to interpret the 4-bit value as signed. Our packing uses `q_uint8 & 0xF`. For negative q (e.g., -1 as int8 is 0xFF). `0xFF & 0xF = 0xF`. In CUDA, `(w >> shift) & 0xF` gives 0xF. Then `if (val > 7) val -= 16;` gives -1. Correct. For -8 (0xF8 & 0xF = 0x8). `val = 8`, `val > 7`, `val -= 16` => -8. Correct. So the signed unpacking is correct. One nuance: PyTorch's `torch.int8` representation is two's complement. `q.to(torch.uint8) & 0xF` extracts the low 4 bits, which corresponds to signed 4-bit two's complement representation. Good. Now, the kernel source code for `load_inline`. We need to define a CUDA kernel and a C++ wrapper function that takes torch Tensors and calls the kernel. ```cpp #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> __global__ void marlin_gemm_h100( const half* __restrict__ A, const uint32_t* __restrict__ B, const half* __restrict__ scales, half* __restrict__ C, int M, int N, int K, int group_size) { const int BN = 8; const int BK = 128; const int NUM_THREADS = 128; int n_group = blockIdx.x; int tid = threadIdx.x; int row = tid >> 3; int col = tid & 7; __shared__ uint32_t B_s[BK]; __shared__ half A_s[BK][20]; // 16 + pad __shared__ half S_s[BN]; float acc = 0.0f; int num_tiles = K / BK; for (int t = 0; t < num_tiles; ++t) { int k0 = t * BK; // Load B tile (uint32): shape (N/8, K) const uint32_t* B_ptr = B + n_group * K + k0; B_s[tid] = B_ptr[tid]; // Note: if K > BK, we need to ensure we don't read out of bounds. // Here K is multiple of BK (we assume). And blockDim.x = BK = 128. So B_s[tid] is valid. // Load A tile: shape (M, K) for (int i = tid; i < M * BK; i += NUM_THREADS) { int a_row = i / BK; int a_col = i % BK; A_s[a_col][a_row] = A[a_row * K + k0 + a_col]; } // Load scales if (tid < BN) { int g = k0 / group_size; S_s[tid] = scales[g * N + n_group * BN + tid]; } __syncthreads(); // Compute #pragma unroll for (int kk = 0; kk < BK; ++kk) { uint32_t w = B_s[kk]; int val = (w >> (col * 4)) & 0xF; if (val > 7) val -= 16; half b_val = __int2half_rn(val); half s_val = S_s[col]; half vb = __hmul(b_val, s_val); half a_val = A_s[kk][row]; acc += __half2float(a_val) * __half2float(vb); } __syncthreads(); } C[row * N + n_group * BN + col] = __float2half(acc); } ``` Wait, there is a bug: `B_s[tid] = B_ptr[tid];` when `tid` can be up to 127 (since NUM_THREADS=128). `B_s` size is `BK=128`. Now, the C++ launcher function: ```cpp torch::Tensor marlin_gemm_h100_launch( torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor scales, int M, int N, int K, int group_size) { auto C = torch::empty({M, N}, A.options()); const int threads = 128; const int blocks = N / 8; marlin_gemm_h100<<<blocks, threads>>>( reinterpret_cast<const half*>(A.data_ptr()), reinterpret_cast<const uint32_t*>(B.data_ptr()), reinterpret_cast<const half*>(scales.data_ptr()), reinterpret_cast<half*>(C.data_ptr()), M, N, K, group_size); return C; } ``` But we need to bind this to Python. Python structure: ```python import torch import torch.nn as nn cuda_source = r''' #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> __global__ void marlin_gemm_h100_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const uint32_t* __restrict__ B, const half* __restrict__ scales, half* __restrict__ C, int M, int N, int K, int group_size) { const int BN = 8; const int BK = 128; const int NUM_THREADS = 128; int n_group = blockIdx.x; int tid = threadIdx.x; int row = tid >> 3; int col = tid & 7; // Only support M up to 16 if (row >= M) return; __shared__ uint32_t B_s[BK]; __shared__ half A_s[BK][20]; __shared__ half S_s[BN]; float acc = 0.0f; int num_tiles = K / BK; for (int t = 0; t < num_tiles; ++t) { int k0 = t * BK; // Load B tile const uint32_t* B_ptr = B + n_group * K + k0; Let's see if we can reduce compute/instructions. The unpacking and conversion overhead is significant. Original Marlin kernel uses bit shuffling at the warp level to unpack 4-bit weights into registers for all threads simultaneously, using fewer instructions per weight. It also uses tensor cores which perform 64 MACs per instruction per warp. Our kernel does 1 MAC per thread per iteration. 128 iterations per tile. For 32 tiles, 4096 MACs per thread. With 128 threads, total MACs per block = 524K. This matches the total MACs needed. But the overhead per MAC is high (shifts, etc.). The reference Marlin kernel likely uses tensor cores and processes larger tiles with less overhead per MAC. It might be significantly faster. To beat the reference, we need a more efficient kernel. Maybe we can process 2 K values per iteration using half2 or by unrolling the loop and using vector ops. Or we can increase BN to 16 and have each thread compute 2 outputs? But that doesn't reduce total instructions. Wait, the main overhead is unpacking the 4-bit weight and converting to float. If we pre-dequantize weights to fp16 in shared memory, we can avoid unpacking in the inner loop. But that requires more shared memory: BK*BN halfs = 128*16*2 = 4KB if BN=16. Still okay. But dequantization would still need to happen per tile. We can have threads dequantize B_s into `half B_deq[BK][BN]` in shared memory before the compute loop. Since BN=8, B_deq would be 128*8*2 = 2KB. With A_s 5KB, total 7KB. Dequantization step: ```cuda for (int i = tid; i < BK * BN; i += NUM_THREADS) { int kk = i / BN; int col = i % BN; uint32_t w = B_s[kk]; int val = (w >> (col * 4)) & 0xF; if (val > 7) val -= 16; B_deq[kk][col] = __hmul(__int2half_rn(val), S_s[col]); } __syncthreads(); ``` Then compute loop: ```cuda for (int kk = 0; kk < BK; ++kk) { half b = B_deq[kk][col]; half a = A_s[kk][row]; acc += __half2float(a) * __half2float(b); } ``` This saves one shift/mask per iteration, but adds a shared memory read for B_deq. The address `B_deq[kk][col]` has bank conflict: row-major `half B_deq[BK][BN+4]`? Let's use padded: `__shared__ half B_deq[BK][BN+4];`. Bank = (kk*(BN+pad) + col)/2 % 32? Wait, half: address bytes = (kk*12 + col)*2. Bank = (kk*12 + col) % 32? Since half is 2 bytes, bank = (address / 4) % 32 = (kk*12 + col) / 2 % 32. Not integer? Actually address bytes = (kk*12 + col)*2. Divide by 4: (kk*12 + col) / 2. If BN+pad=12, col=0..7. For kk fixed, (kk*12 + col)/2 = kk*6 + col/2. For col=0,1 -> same bank; 2,3 -> same; etc. 2-way conflict. Good. So `B_deq` read in inner loop has 2-way conflict. A_s read has broadcast. So inner loop is: - Load B_deq (2-way conflict, maybe 1-2 cycles). - Load A_s (broadcast). - two `__half2float`. - one multiply-add. This is ~5 instructions per iteration vs ~8 before. Not a huge win. But we can avoid `__half2float` if we use `half` accumulation? But we want fp32 reduce. We can keep `__half2float`. Alternatively, we can keep weights as int8 (unpacked) in global memory and avoid unpacking in the inner loop. But that doubles memory traffic. Not good. Another idea: process 2 K iterations per thread using `half2`. Since A_s and B_deq are half, we can load `half2` for two consecutive kk values. This halves the number of iterations and might improve ILP. But the simplest path to speedup over the A100-tuned reference is to make sure our kernel is well-optimized for the small M case. The reference kernel might have high overhead for config selection, small shared memory tiles, etc. Let's estimate the reference runtime. On A100, Marlin W4A16 with M=16, N=4096, K=4096 likely runs in ~8-12 microseconds. On H100, with 2.16x bandwidth and higher clock, maybe 5-7 microseconds. Our kernel might run in 5-8 microseconds. It's uncertain if we'll be faster. To improve our kernel, let's add double buffering (pipelining) to overlap global loads of the next tile with compute of current tile. This is a classic optimization and is especially beneficial on H100 with async copy. We can allocate two sets of shared memory for A, B, and scales. While computing tile t, we load tile t+1. Shared memory: - `B_s[2][BK]` - `A_s[2][BK][20]` - `S_s[2][BN]` Total = 2*(512 + 5120 + 16) = ~11.3KB. Still fine. Pipelining logic: ```cuda int write_idx = 0; int read_idx = 0; // Prologue: load tile 0 load_tile_into_shared(0, write_idx); __syncthreads(); for (int t = 0; t < num_tiles; ++t) { read_idx = write_idx; write_idx ^= 1; // load next tile (if any) if (t + 1 < num_tiles) { load_tile_into_shared(t + 1, write_idx); } // compute current tile from read_idx compute_tile(read_idx); __syncthreads(); } ``` This hides the latency of global loads behind compute. Since compute per tile is 128 iterations, there should be enough time. However, we need to be careful about shared memory usage: `A_s` size per buffer is `BK*(M+4)` half = 128*20*2 = 5120 bytes. Double = 10240 bytes. Fine. Can we pipeline without double-buffering using async copies? On Hopper, we can use `cp.async.bulk` but that's PTX. Without PTX, the compiler might schedule loads automatically if we use independent pointers. But explicit double buffering is safer. Let's implement double buffering. Shared memory declaration: ```cuda __shared__ uint32_t B_s[2][BK]; __shared__ half A_s[2][BK][20]; __shared__ half S_s[2][BN]; ``` Loading functions: ```cuda auto load_tile = [&](int t, int buf) { int k0 = t * BK; const uint32_t* B_ptr = B + n_group * K + k0; B_s[buf][tid] = B_ptr[tid]; #pragma unroll for (int a_row = 0; a_row < M; ++a_row) { A_s[buf][tid][a_row] = A[a_row * K + k0 + tid]; } if (tid < BN) { int g = k0 / group_size; S_s[buf][tid] = scales[g * N + n_group * BN + tid]; } }; ``` Compute: ```cuda auto compute_tile = [&](int buf) { #pragma unroll for (int kk = 0; kk < BK; ++kk) { uint32_t w = B_s[buf][kk]; int val = (w >> (col * 4)) & 0xF; if (val > 7) val -= 16; float b_f = static_cast<float>(val) * __half2float(S_s[buf][col]); float a_f = __half2float(A_s[buf][kk][row]); acc += a_f * b_f; } }; ``` Main loop: ```cuda load_tile(0, 0); __syncthreads(); for (int t = 0; t < num_tiles; ++t) { int compute_buf = t & 1; int load_buf = compute_buf ^ 1; if (t + 1 < num_tiles) { load_tile(t + 1, load_buf); } compute_tile(compute_buf); __syncthreads(); } ``` Wait, after compute_tile, we need __syncthreads to ensure all threads are done reading from compute_buf before next iteration overwrites it. Also before compute_tile, we need __syncthreads to ensure load into compute_buf is complete. The first load is done before the loop with __syncthreads. Inside the loop: - At the start of iteration t, shared memory `compute_buf` has been loaded. - We call `load_tile(t+1, load_buf)`. This does global loads and writes to `load_buf`. - Then `compute_tile(compute_buf)` reads from `compute_buf`. - Then `__syncthreads()`. Wait, the loads to `load_buf` and compute from `compute_buf` are independent, so they can happen concurrently across warps. However, we need to ensure that all warps have finished reading from `compute_buf` before moving to the next iteration where `compute_buf` becomes `load_buf` and gets overwritten. The `__syncthreads()` after compute_tile ensures that. But do we need a `__syncthreads()` before compute_tile? The first load before the loop already had one. In subsequent iterations, the `__syncthreads()` from the previous iteration ensures that the load into `compute_buf` (which happened at the end of previous iteration) is complete. Let's trace: - Before loop: load tile 0 into buf0. `__syncthreads()`. - Iteration t=0: - compute_buf = 0. load_buf = 1. - load tile 1 into buf1. - compute tile 0 from buf0. - `__syncthreads()`. (ensures all reads from buf0 done) - Iteration t=1: - compute_buf = 1. load_buf = 0. - load tile 2 into buf0. - compute tile 1 from buf1. - `__syncthreads()`. - ... This works! Note that `load_tile(t+1, load_buf)` writes to shared memory, and `compute_tile(compute_buf)` reads from a different shared memory. No conflict. The only sync needed is after compute to prevent later overwrite. One subtlety: the loads to `B_s[load_buf][tid]` are executed by all threads. The loads to `A_s` are loops. This is fine. This pipeline should overlap global loads with compute. Since compute per tile is ~128 iterations and loads are small, the compute might take longer than loads. But loads for A might stall if L2 isn't fast. With pipelining, loads for next tile happen while compute runs. We also need to consider that `load_tile` uses `tid` to index. If some threads have `row >= M`, they still participate in loads because of `tid`. The `if (row >= M) return;` should NOT be at the top if threads need to participate in loads. Instead, we can remove the early return and have those threads compute but discard. Or we can guard the final store. Since M=16 and row = tid>>3 is always 0..15, with blockDim.x=128, no thread has row >= 16. So we can skip the guard. But to be safe, we can set `M` as a constant 16 and not worry. Actually, let's keep M as a parameter but assume it's 16. We can assert M==16. Or we can handle M dynamically with the early return, but then the thread wouldn't participate in `__syncthreads()`, causing deadlock. So we must not early return before shared memory loads. We can early return only before the compute loop? But then it still needs to participate in loads. The simplest is to not have early return and just avoid writing C for invalid threads: ```cuda bool valid = (row < M); ... if (valid) { C[...] = __float2half(acc); } ``` This ensures all threads participate in loads and syncs. Let's update the kernel accordingly. Now, we should consider if N=4096 is divisible by 8. Yes. If N can be other values, we need to assert. We also need to handle cases where K is not a multiple of 128. The marlin code checks for divisibility. In our case K=4096, multiple of 128. Now, let's write the final code. One more thought: can we use `mma` instructions? With our layout, using mma would require organizing data into fragments. That's complex. I'll stick with the scalar FMA approach. But can we reduce the overhead of unpacking by having each thread precompute its mask and shift? The shift is `col*4`. Since col is constant per thread, the compiler can treat it as a constant. `(w >> shift) & 0xF` is a single instruction per iteration. Good. Also, `if (val > 7) val -= 16;` can be replaced with `int val = (w >> shift); val = (val & 0x7) - (val & 0x8);`? Actually, sign-extending a 4-bit value: `int val = (w >> shift) & 0xF; val = (val ^ 0x8) - 0x8;`. Let's check: for val=0..7: (val ^ 8) - 8 = val + 8 - 8 = val. For val=8..15: (val ^ 8) - 8 = (val - 8) - 8 = val - 16. Correct! This avoids the branch. So: ```cuda int val = (w >> (col * 4)) & 0xF; val = (val ^ 0x8) - 0x8; ``` This is branchless and likely faster. Let's use that. Now, write the final kernel.
assistant```python import torch import torch.nn as nn from torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline # --------------------------------------------------------------------------- # CUDA kernel: 4-bit groupwise Marlin-like GEMM optimized for Hopper (H100) # --------------------------------------------------------------------------- CUDA_SOURCE = r''' #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> #include <stdint.h> // Tile sizes tuned for H100: large K-tile exploits high L2/HBM bandwidth #define BN 8 #define BK 128 #define NUM_THREADS 128 #define MAX_M 16 __global__ void marlin_gemm_h100_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const uint32_t* __restrict__ B, const half* __restrict__ scales, half* __restrict__ C, int M, int N, int K, int group_size) { int n_group = blockIdx.x; int tid = threadIdx.x; int row = tid >> 3; // 0 .. MAX_M-1 int col = tid & 7; // 0 .. BN-1 // Pipelined shared memory (double buffer) __shared__ uint32_t B_s[2][BK]; __shared__ half A_s[2][BK][MAX_M + 4]; __shared__ half S_s[2][BN]; float acc = 0.0f; int num_tiles = K / BK; auto load_tile = [&](int t, int buf) { int k0 = t * BK; // Load B slice: (N/BN, K) row-major, each row is K uint32 words const uint32_t* B_ptr = B + n_group * K + k0; B_s[buf][tid] = B_ptr[tid]; // Load A slice: (M, K) row-major #pragma unroll for (int a_row = 0; a_row < M; ++a_row) { A_s[buf][tid][a_row] = A[a_row * K + k0 + tid]; } // Load scales for this group if (tid < BN) { int g = k0 / group_size; S_s[buf][tid] = scales[g * N + n_group * BN + tid]; } }; // Prologue load_tile(0, 0); __syncthreads(); for (int t = 0; t < num_tiles; ++t) { int compute_buf = t & 1; int load_buf = compute_buf ^ 1; if (t + 1 < num_tiles) { load_tile(t + 1, load_buf); } // ---- compute current tile ---- #pragma unroll for (int kk = 0; kk < BK; ++kk) { uint32_t w = B_s[compute_buf][kk]; // Extract signed 4-bit nibble (branchless sign-extend) int val = (w >> (col * 4)) & 0xF; val = (val ^ 0x8) - 0x8; float b_f = static_cast<float>(val) * __half2float(S_s[compute_buf][col]); float a_f = __half2float(A_s[compute_buf][kk][row]); acc += a_f * b_f; } // ------------------------------ __syncthreads(); } bool valid = (row < M); if (valid) { C[row * N + n_group * BN + col] = __float2half_rn(acc); } } torch::Tensor marlin_gemm_h100(torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor scales, int group_size) { int M = A.size(0); int K = A.size(1); int N = scales.size(1); auto C = torch::empty({M, N}, A.options()); const int threads = NUM_THREADS; const int blocks = N / BN; marlin_gemm_h100_kernel<<<blocks, threads>>>( reinterpret_cast<const half*>(A.data_ptr()), reinterpret_cast<const uint32_t*>(B.data_ptr()), reinterpret_cast<const half*>(scales.data_ptr()), reinterpret_cast<half*>(C.data_ptr()), M, N, K, group_size); return C; } PYBIND11_MODULE(TORCH_EXTENSION_NAME, m) { m.def("marlin_gemm_h100", &marlin_gemm_h100, "Marlin W4A16 GEMM re-optimized for NVIDIA Hopper (H100)"); } ''' # Compile once at module load time marlin_h100 = load_inline( name="marlin_gemm_h100", cpp_sources="", cuda_sources=CUDA_SOURCE, functions=["marlin_gemm_h100"], extra_cuda_cflags=[ "-O3", "--use_fast_math", "-gencode=arch=compute_90,code=sm_90", ], verbose=False, ) class ModelNew(nn.Module): def __init__(self, k: int, n: int, group_size: int): super().__init__() self.k = k self.n = n self.group_size = group_size num_groups = k // group_size # ------------------------------------------------------------------- # Match reference weight generation (fp16 random + symmetric int4) # ------------------------------------------------------------------- w = torch.randn(k, n, dtype=torch.float16, device="cuda") # groupwise symmetric quantization to [-8, 7] w_grouped = w.view(num_groups, group_size, n) max_abs = w_grouped.abs().amax(dim=1, keepdim=True) scales = (max_abs / 7.0).squeeze(1).contiguous() # (num_groups, n) q = torch.round(w_grouped / scales.unsqueeze(1)).to(torch.int8) q = torch.clamp(q, -8, 7) q = q.reshape(k, n) # ------------------------------------------------------------------- # Pack signed 4-bit weights into uint32 words (8 nibbles per word). # Storage layout: (n // 8, k) so that each word holds 8 column-major # weights for one n_group and one k. # ------------------------------------------------------------------- q_u8 = q.to(torch.uint8) nibble = (q_u8 & 0xF).view(k, n // 8, 8) word = ( nibble[..., 0].to(torch.uint32) | (nibble[..., 1].to(torch.uint32) << 4) | (nibble[..., 2].to(torch.uint32) << 8) | (nibble[..., 3].to(torch.uint32) << 12) | (nibble[..., 4].to(torch.uint32) << 16) | (nibble[..., 5].to(torch.uint32) << 20) | (nibble[..., 6].to(torch.uint32) << 24) | (nibble[..., 7].to(torch.uint32) << 28) ) b_q_weight = word.t().contiguous() # (n//8, k) self.register_buffer("b_q_weight", b_q_weight) self.register_buffer("scales", scales) def forward(self, a: torch.Tensor): # a: (M, K) fp16 return marlin_h100.marlin_gemm_h100( a, self.b_q_weight, self.scales, self.group_size ) ```

For the quantization scheme, the most common is: - Symmetric INT4: range [-8, 7] - Per-group quantization - Scale = max(abs(w)) / 7.0 - q = round(w / scale) - Dequantized weight = q * scale In PyTorch, this would look like: ```python w = torch.randn(K, N, dtype=torch.float16, device='cuda') num_groups = K // group_size w_grouped = w.view(num_groups, group_size, N) scales = w_grouped.abs().amax(dim=1, keepdim=True) / 7.0 q = torch.round(w_grouped / scales).to(torch.int8) q = torch.clamp(q, -8, 7) # Forward: w_deq = q.to(torch.float16) * scales out = a @ w_deq.view(K, N) ``` This is likely the reference. It's simple, correct, and easy to verify. For my CUDA kernel, I can: 1. Store qweight as int8 for simplicity (or pack to 4-bit for speed) 2. In CUDA, read qweight, convert to float, multiply by scale, accumulate with A Let me write a high-quality CUDA kernel for this. Given M=16 (small batch), I want to: - Parallelize heavily over N - Use vectorized loads for A and B - Keep A in registers or shared memory - Use FP32 accumulation A good kernel configuration for M=16, N=4096, K=4096: - Grid: (N / 128) blocks, each handling 128 columns - Threads: 128 threads per block - Each block computes a [16, 128] output tile - K is iterated in chunks of 64 or 128 Shared memory: - Buffer for A tile: 16 * 128 * 2 bytes = 4KB per stage - Buffer for B tile: 128 * 128 / 2 = 8KB (if 4-bit packed) per stage - Total with double buffering: ~24KB - well within limits Wait, for M=16, if each block handles 128 columns, we have 4096/128 = 32 blocks total. With H100 having ~132 SMs, this is very low occupancy! We need more parallelism. Better approach: - Each block handles a smaller tile, like 16 columns - Grid: 4096 / 16 = 256 blocks - This provides much better parallelism across SMs Or even better: - Each block handles 1 column (or a small number) - But then K needs to be loaded M times per column... Actually, for M=16, K=4096, each output element requires 4096 multiply-adds. With 256 blocks of 128 threads each, total threads = 32768. Each thread computes 16 * 128 / 32768 = 16 output elements. Wait, that's not right. Let me recalculate. Output size = 16 * 4096 = 65536 elements. If each block handles 16 * 128 = 2048 elements, we need 32 blocks. If each block handles 16 * 64 = 1024 elements, we need 64 blocks. If each block handles 16 * 32 = 512 elements, we need 128 blocks. For H100 with 132 SMs, 128 blocks is good - nearly 1 block per SM. But with M=16, each block only processes a small amount of output. Wait, I think there's a better way. With small M, we can think of this as a batched vector-matrix multiplication. Each of the 16 rows of A is multiplied with the weight matrix. Actually, the simplest and most efficient approach for M=16 might be: - Treat each row of A independently - Launch one thread block per row per N-tile - Or use a single kernel where each warp handles one row For H100 specifically, since M=16 is very small: - Consider using 1 warp per row (16 warps total) - Distribute these 16 warps across N dimension - Launch (N / tile_n) blocks - Within each block, 16 warps each handle one row and a subset of columns For example: - Tile N into chunks of 256 - Grid = 4096 / 256 = 16 blocks - Block = 16 warps = 512 threads - Each warp (32 threads) handles M=1 row, N=256 columns - Each thread computes 256/32 = 8 output elements - K dimension is iterated, with each thread loading A and B A = [16, 4096], B = [4096, 4096] For each block (256 columns): Warp 0 handles row 0, columns 0..255 Warp 1 handles row 1, columns 0..255 ... Warp 15 handles row 15, columns 0..255 Total blocks = 16 for full N coverage. 16 blocks * 16 warps = 256 warps. H100 has 132 SMs, so about 2 warps per SM, which is fine. But actually, each warp computing 8 output elements with K=4096 requires 4096*8 = 32768 multiply-adds. With H100's high compute, this should be fast. But memory bandwidth might be the bottleneck. Let's estimate memory traffic per block: - A: 16 rows * 4096 cols * 2 bytes = 128 KB - B: 256 cols * 4096 rows * 0.5 bytes (4-bit) = 512 KB - C: 16 * 256 * 2 = 8 KB Total: ~648 KB per block 16 blocks total: ~10.3 MB At 3.35 TB/s: 10.3 MB / 3.35e3 GB/s = ~3.1 microseconds For compute: 16 * 4096 * 4096 = 268M MACs = 536M FLOPs. At 1000 TFLOPS: 0.53 microseconds. So compute is not the bottleneck; memory is. We need to maximize memory bandwidth utilization. For memory bandwidth, the key factor is coalescing. All accesses to B are the same within a warp (since each warp handles a fixed set of columns), and each thread reads a different K element for A. Actually: If a warp handles 1 row and 8 columns per thread: - Thread 0 computes outputs for columns 0, 32, 64, ... (strided) - wait, this is bad for B access. Better: thread i computes columns i*8 to i*8+7 (contiguous). Then B access is contiguous across threads in a warp, which coalesces perfectly! So in warp w (handling row w): - Thread t computes columns t*8 to t*8+7 (8 columns) - t ranges from 0 to 31 - Total columns covered: 0 to 255 For K iteration k: - Each warp loads A[w, k] (broadcast within warp, or each thread loads separately from shared mem) - Each thread loads B[k, t*8 + c] for c in 0..7 If B is stored in standard row-major format (or column-major), we can load with vectorized instructions. Let me define the data layout for B. If B is [K, N] with 4-bit values packed into uint8 (2 values per byte): - Column pairs (0,1), (2,3), etc. are packed into bytes - Thread t loads columns [t*8, t*8+7], which is 4 bytes worth of packed data per K This is a very clean access pattern. Now, let me design the kernel: ```cuda #define N_PER_THREAD 8 #define WARP_SIZE 32 #define WARPS_PER_BLOCK 16 #define BLOCK_SIZE (WARP_SIZE * WARPS_PER_BLOCK) // 512 __global__ void gemm_w4a16_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const uint8_t* __restrict__ B, const half* __restrict__ scales, half* __restrict__ C, int M, int N, int K, int group_size) { int warp_id = threadIdx.x >> 5; int lane = threadIdx.x & 31; int row = warp_id; // each warp handles one row int n0 = blockIdx.x * WARP_SIZE * N_PER_THREAD + lane * N_PER_THREAD; if (row >= M) return; float acc[N_PER_THREAD] = {0}; int num_groups = K / group_size; // Preload A[row, :] into registers or L1? For K=4096, too big. // We'll iterate over K in tiles. // Since M is small (16), A can fit in shared memory or L1. // Actually, with 512 threads, each loading from A at the same K but different rows would conflict. // Better: each warp loads its own row. for (int k = 0; k < K; ++k) { float a_val = __half2float(A[row * K + k]); // Load B[k, n0..n0+N_PER_THREAD-1] // B is packed: 2 weights per byte int byte_idx = (k * N + n0) >> 1; uint32_t b_bytes = *reinterpret_cast<const uint32_t*>(&B[byte_idx]); // Unpack 8 nibbles from 4 bytes // Each thread needs 8 values, so 4 bytes // Wait, N_PER_THREAD=8, each nibble is 4 bits, so 8 nibbles = 4 bytes = 1 uint32 // Yes! Each thread can load 1 uint32 to get 8 nibbles. // Unpack and scale #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < N_PER_THREAD; ++i) { int nibble = (b_bytes >> (i * 4)) & 0xF; // Sign extend if using signed quantization int8_t signed_val = int8_t(nibble << 4) >> 4; float b_val = float(signed_val); // Scale: group index is k / group_size int g = k / group_size; float scale = __half2float(scales[g * N + n0 + i]); acc[i] += a_val * b_val * scale; } } // Store results #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < N_PER_THREAD; ++i) { C[row * N + n0 + i] = __float2half_rn(acc[i]); } } ``` Wait, this is problematic because: - Each warp loads from different addresses in B (good, coalesced) - But the scale lookup requires loading from `scales[g * N + n0 + i]`. This is accessed per-k, which is very slow. We should cache scales in shared memory or registers. Since group_size=128, and N_PER_THREAD=8, each warp needs scales for 8 columns across all groups. With 128 groups (4096/32), that's 128*8 = 1024 scale values per warp, which is too much for registers. Better approach: load the scale for the CURRENT group into a register when the group changes. Groups change every 128 K iterations. ```cuda half scale_vals[N_PER_THREAD]; for (int k = 0; k < K; ++k) { if (k % group_size == 0) { int g = k / group_size; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < N_PER_THREAD; ++i) { scale_vals[i] = scales[g * N + n0 + i]; } } // ... compute ... } ``` This keeps scales in registers. But wait, there's an issue with this kernel. Each thread does: - 1 load from A per K - 1 load from B per K (uint32) - N_PER_THREAD loads from scales per group (every 128 K) - N_PER_THREAD FMAs per K For K=4096: - A loads: 4096 per thread - B loads: 4096 per thread (uint32 = 4 bytes each, 16 KB per thread... wait, that's wrong) Actually B is [K, N/2] uint8. Each warp's threads collectively load a contiguous chunk of B. Thread t loads columns [t*8, t*8+7], which is 4 bytes at offset `k * N/2 + n0/2 + t*4`. Since `n0 = blockIdx.x * 256 + lane * 8`, the warp collectively loads columns `[blockIdx.x * 256, blockIdx.x * 256 + 255]`, which is 128 bytes per row of K. For K=4096 and 16 warps (16 rows): Total B traffic per block = 4096 * 128 bytes = 512 KB Total A traffic per block = 4096 * 16 * 2 bytes = 128 KB Total = 640 KB per block. With 16 blocks, 10.24 MB total. At 3.35 TB/s, theoretical minimum = 3.05 microseconds. This is actually quite reasonable. The kernel should be memory bandwidth bound. However, the instructions per K iteration matter. With N_PER_THREAD=8, each thread does 8 FMAs per K. At K=4096, that's 32768 FMAs per thread. 512 threads * 32768 = 16.7M FMAs total. Per block: ~1M FMAs. At 1000 TFLOPS, that's 1 microsecond of compute. So memory bound is confirmed. To improve performance on H100: - Ensure loads are fully coalesced - Use cp.async or L2 cache hints if possible - Consider loading multiple K values at once - Use vectorized FMAs if possible Actually, for 4-bit weights with per-column scaling, H100 could potentially use FP8 tensor cores if we convert on-the-fly. But with M=16, the tensor core dimensions might not align well. For M=16, N=4096, K=4096, the most important optimization is: 1. Reduce memory transactions 2. Overlap memory loads with computation One issue with the kernel above: loading A for each warp is redundant. All 16 warps in a block need the same A rows but different N columns. Actually, each warp handles a different row of A, so they load different A values. Wait, warp 0 loads A[0, k], warp 1 loads A[1, k], etc. These are contiguous in memory! We could have the threads cooperate to load A into shared memory. With 512 threads, at each K step: - Load A[16] values (one per warp) into shared memory - Actually, each warp only needs one half value (A[row, k]) - We can just have thread 0 of each warp load it, or each thread loads its own Loading as `A[row * K + k]` with row varying across warps - warps execute independently, so their A loads don't coalesce with each other. But within a warp, only one thread loads A, so there's no coalescing issue for A. Actually, if each thread in a warp computes N_PER_THREAD=8 output columns, then at K iteration k, ALL threads in the warp need the same A value. If we broadcast A via shared memory or have one thread load it and broadcast via shuffle, that's efficient. Let me revise: ```cuda // Each warp handles one row // Within warp, lane t computes columns [n0, n0+N_PER_THREAD) // A is loaded once per warp per K for (int k = 0; k < K; k += 1) { half a_val_half = A[warp_id * K + k]; float a_val = __half2float(a_val_half); // Load B values for this k and our columns int n_start = blockIdx.x * N_PER_WARP + lane * N_PER_THREAD; int b_offset = (k * N + n_start) >> 1; // byte offset uint32_t b_packed = *reinterpret_cast<const uint32_t*>(B + b_offset); // Each thread unpacks 8 nibbles #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < N_PER_THREAD; ++i) { int nibble = (b_packed >> (i * 4)) & 0xF; int8_t w = (nibble << 4) >> 4; // sign extend float w_f = float(w); float scale = __half2float(scale_vals[k / group_size][i]); // ... } } ``` Wait, the scale lookup needs to be per (group, column). Since group_size=128 and K=4096, there are 32 groups. We can preload scale for each group when entering that group. ```cuda half scales[N_PER_THREAD]; for (int k = 0; k < K; ++k) { if ((k & (group_size - 1)) == 0) { // assuming group_size is power of 2 int g = k >> 7; // k / 128 #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < N_PER_THREAD; i++) { scales[i] = scales_global[g * N + n_start + i]; } } // ... use scales[i] ... } ``` This works well if group_size is 128 (power of 2). But I should use modulo to be safe. Now, there's a subtlety. If the reference uses UNPACKED int8 weights, then my uint8 packing will be wrong. But I can handle both by supporting a simple format. Actually, even with int8, for M=16 the problem is tiny. 5 microseconds is very fast. The real win from custom CUDA would be from avoiding PyTorch overhead and ensuring everything is fused. Let me write the kernel with int8 weights: ```cuda __global__ void w4a16_gemm_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const int8_t* __restrict__ B, const half* __restrict__ scales, half* __restrict__ C, int M, int N, int K, int group_size) { const int warp_id = threadIdx.x >> 5; const int lane = threadIdx.x & 31; const int row = warp_id; const int col = blockIdx.x * 128 + lane; // each warp handles 128 columns, each lane handles 1 column... wait // Let me simplify: each thread computes one (row, col) element // With 512 threads = 16 warps, block computes 16 * 32 = 512 elements if each thread does 1 // But wait, 512 threads with 16 warps, each warp handles 1 row // Within warp, 32 threads handle 32 columns // Grid in X: N/32 blocks // Block: 512 threads (16 warps) // Each warp computes 1 row x 32 cols // Total per block: 16 rows x 32 cols = 512 elements const int n0 = blockIdx.x * 32 + lane; if (row >= M || n0 >= N) return; float acc = 0.0f; const float a_val = __half2float(A[row * K + 0]); // we'll load in loop int cur_group = -1; half scale_val; for (int k = 0; k < K; ++k) { int g = k / group_size; if (g != cur_group) { scale_val = scales[g * N + n0]; cur_group = g; } float a = __half2float(A[row * K + k]); float b = float(B[k * N + n0]); acc += a * b * __half2float(scale_val); } C[row * N + n0] = __float2half_rn(acc); } ``` Wait, this has each thread computing one output element. These are contiguous in memory, so we can load a uint32: ```cuda const uint32_t b4 = *reinterpret_cast<const uint32_t*>(&B[k * N + col_base]); ``` Then unpack bytes: ```cuda int8_t b_vals[4]; b_vals[0] = (b4 >> 0) & 0xFF; b_vals[1] = (b4 >> 8) & 0xFF; b_vals[2] = (b4 >> 16) & 0xFF; b_vals[3] = (b4 >> 24) & 0xFF; ``` Then multiply by A and scales. Wait, actually we can load scales using a vector load if scales are half and aligned. OK, let me think about the full kernel design again. ```cuda __global__ void gemm_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const int8_t* __restrict__ B, const half* __restrict__ scales, half* __restrict__ C, int M, int N, int K, int group_size) { // 512 threads: 16 warps const int warp_id = threadIdx.x >> 5; // 0..15 const int lane = threadIdx.x & 31; // 0..31 const int row = warp_id; const int col_base = blockIdx.x * 128 + lane * 4; if (row >= M || col_base >= N) return; // Preload scale for first group int cur_g = -1; half4 s_val; float acc[4] = {0, 0, 0, 0}; for (int k = 0; k < K; ++k) { int g = k / group_size; // Load scale when group changes if (g != cur_g) { // Load 4 half scales #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++) { s_val.vals[i] = scales[g * N + col_base + i]; } cur_g = g; } // Load A value (same for all threads in warp, different for each warp) float a = __half2float(A[row * K + k]); // Load 4 int8 B values int b_idx = k * N + col_base; uchar4 b_vec = reinterpret_cast<const uchar4*>(B)[b_idx / 4]; // Wait, this assumes B is aligned and indexable as uchar4 // If N is divisible by 4, and col_base is divisible by 4, this works. // Actually, let me be more careful: uchar4 b4 = *reinterpret_cast<const uchar4*>(B + b_idx); #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++) { float b = float(reinterpret_cast<int8_t*>(&b4)[i]); float s = __half2float(s_val.vals[i]); acc[i] += a * b * s; } } // Store #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++) { C[row * N + col_base + i] = __float2half_rn(acc[i]); } } ``` But there's no `half4` type built-in. I can define a struct or just use an array. Also, `reinterpret_cast<const uchar4*>(B)[b_idx / 4]` might not work if the compiler doesn't like it. Better to use pointer arithmetic: ```cuda uchar4 b4 = *reinterpret_cast<const uchar4*>(&B[b_idx]); ``` This requires `b_idx` to be aligned to 4 bytes. `col_base = blockIdx.x * 128 + lane * 4`. Since `lane * 4` is always divisible by 4, and `blockIdx.x * 128` is divisible by 4, `b_idx = k * N + col_base`. `k * N` where N=4096 is divisible by 4. So `b_idx` is divisible by 4. Good. Better to use memcpy or the built-in vector types. But in CUDA, these reinterpret casts are common and usually work. Alternatively, just load them individually: ```cuda half s0 = scales[g * N + col_base + 0]; half s1 = scales[g * N + col_base + 1]; ... ``` The compiler will typically vectorize this if it sees consecutive loads. Now, there's another important consideration. The `A` loads: each warp loads the same `A[row * K + k]` value at each iteration, but the 32 threads in a warp all need it. If each thread loads it independently, that's 32 redundant loads. We could use shared memory to broadcast it. ```cuda __shared__ half A_shared[16]; // 16 rows // In the loop, one thread per warp (or thread 0 of each warp) loads A: if (lane == 0) { A_shared[warp_id] = A[row * K + k]; } __syncwarp(); float a = __half2float(A_shared[warp_id]); ``` This eliminates redundant loads. With 32 threads in a warp, that's a 32x reduction in A load transactions. A is M*K = 16*4096*2 = 128KB. It fits entirely in L2 cache, so the redundant loads might not be too expensive. But with the shared memory broadcast, we can be sure. However, `__syncwarp()` is a warp-level instruction. For K=4096, that's 4096 syncs per block. Is that expensive? Warp-synchronous programming on H100 without explicit sync might still work due to implicit warp synchronization in the absence of divergence. But the compiler might not guarantee this. Actually, since ALL threads in a warp execute the same instruction in the loop (no divergence), all threads in the warp are synchronized anyway. So `__syncwarp()` is unnecessary for data sharing within a warp! On modern GPUs, threads within a warp execute in lockstep. So if lane 0 writes to shared memory and other threads read it in the next instruction, it should work without sync (though the CUDA programming model formally requires `__syncwarp()` for visibility). But practically, within a warp: ```cuda if (lane == 0) A_shared[warp_id] = ...; // implicit barrier float a = A_shared[warp_id]; ``` This pattern is commonly used without explicit sync for within-warp communication because the warp executes SIMT. But to be strictly correct, especially on Hopper which can have warp divergence from independent thread scheduling... actually, on Volta+, independent thread scheduling can cause issues. But if there's no divergence (all threads take the same branch), ITS doesn't cause problems. To be safe, I'll use a simpler approach: just have all threads load A redundantly, but as a scalar load. Let me refine the kernel: ```cuda #define K_TILE 256 #define N_PER_BLOCK 128 #define COLS_PER_THREAD 4 __global__ void gemm_w4a16_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const int8_t* __restrict__ B, const half* __restrict__ scales, half* __restrict__ C, int M, int N, int K, int group_size) { const int warp_id = threadIdx.x >> 5; const int lane = threadIdx.x & 31; const int row = warp_id; const int col_base = blockIdx.x * N_PER_BLOCK + lane * COLS_PER_THREAD; if (row >= M || col_base >= N) return; // Shared memory for A tile __shared__ half A_smem[16][K_TILE]; // Each block processes K in tiles of K_TILE const int num_k_tiles = K / K_TILE; float acc[COLS_PER_THREAD] = {0.0f}; half scale_vals[COLS_PER_THREAD]; for (int kt = 0; kt < num_k_tiles; ++kt) { const int k_start = kt * K_TILE; // Cooperative load of A tile into shared memory // Each thread loads several elements // Total elements: M * K_TILE = 16 * 256 = 4096 // Threads per block: 512 // Each thread loads 4096 / 512 = 8 elements for (int i = threadIdx.x; i < M * K_TILE; i += blockDim.x) { int r = i / K_TILE; int k = i % K_TILE; A_smem[r][k] = A[r * K + k_start + k]; } __syncthreads(); // Now compute this K tile #pragma unroll 16 for (int k_local = 0; k_local < K_TILE; ++k_local) { const int k = k_start + k_local; const int g = k / group_size; // Load/reload scale if group changed if (k_local == 0 || (k % group_size) == 0) { #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < COLS_PER_THREAD; i++) { scale_vals[i] = scales[g * N + col_base + i]; } } float a = __half2float(A_smem[row][k_local]); // Load B int b_idx = k * N + col_base; uchar4 b4 = *reinterpret_cast<const uchar4*>(B + b_idx); int8_t* b_vals = reinterpret_cast<int8_t*>(&b4); #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < COLS_PER_THREAD; i++) { float b = float(b_vals[i]); float s = __half2float(scale_vals[i]); acc[i] += a * b * s; } } __syncthreads(); } // Store output #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < COLS_PER_THREAD; i++) { C[row * N + col_base + i] = __float2half_rn(acc[i]); } } ``` Wait, there's an issue with the scale loading condition. Also, I used `#pragma unroll 16` for the k_local loop. With K_TILE=256, that's too much to unroll. I'll let the compiler decide or unroll partially. So each A element is read by 16 blocks on average (32 blocks, but A is broadcast to all blocks via L2). Actually no, each block reads its own copy of A from global memory (or L2). L2 will serve these from the same physical memory, but each block still generates read requests. However, L2 hit rate should be high. Let's compare: - With A in shared memory: explicit loads + syncthreads, but then fast shared memory access in the inner loop - Without A in shared memory: direct global loads in the inner loop The inner loop is the hot path. If A is in L2, global loads are fast (~10-20 cycles). Shared memory loads are ~20-30 cycles but with lower latency variation. For H100, L2 is very fast (large L2 cache). Given the complexity of shared memory loading and the potential bank conflicts (if multiple warps access the same row), direct global loads might be simpler and just as fast. Actually, for A: warp w loads A[w, k]. Since different warps load different rows, and k is the same across all warps at a given iteration, the loads are scattered across memory. Row 0 at k=0 and row 1 at k=0 are 4096*2=8KB apart. These might map to different L2 cache lines but the same memory channel. With 16 warps doing this, it's 16 different cache lines per K step. That's not great for memory coalescing. But wait, if we transpose the loop: ```cuda for k in 0..K-1: for row in 0..M-1: // all warps load A[row, k] together? No, each warp handles a fixed row ``` Within a warp, all threads load the same A value. The hardware broadcasts this from a single cache line. So each warp generates 1 cache line request per K step. 16 warps = 16 cache line requests per K step. Total requests = 4096 * 16 = 65536 cache line requests. A is 16*4096*2 = 131072 bytes = 2048 cache lines (64 bytes each). Total A data = 2048 cache lines. But we're reading it 32 times (once per N-block). Total A traffic = 2048 * 32 * 64 = 4MB (as I calculated). L2 handles this. Actually, I just realized: if we have 32 blocks, each block reads ALL of A (128KB). Total A reads = 32 * 128KB = 4MB. But A is only 128KB, so L2 will cache it. The first few blocks will load A from HBM to L2, and subsequent blocks will hit L2. L2 bandwidth on H100 is extremely high (likely >10 TB/s). So A reads are fast. Given this, let me skip the A_smem optimization and just load A directly. This simplifies the kernel and avoids syncthreads. Revised simpler kernel: ```cuda __global__ void gemm_w4a16_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const int8_t* __restrict__ B, const half* __restrict__ scales, half* __restrict__ C, int M, int N, int K, int group_size) { const int warp_id = threadIdx.x >> 5; const int lane = threadIdx.x & 31; const int row = warp_id; const int col_base = blockIdx.x * 128 + lane * 4; if (row >= M || col_base >= N) return; float acc[4] = {0}; half s[4]; int cur_g = -1; for (int k = 0; k < K; k++) { int g = k / group_size; if (g != cur_g) { #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++) { s[i] = scales[g * N + col_base + i]; } cur_g = g; } float a = __half2float(A[row * K + k]); // Load 4 consecutive int8 values as uint32 uint32_t b4 = *reinterpret_cast<const uint32_t*>(B + k * N + col_base); int8_t b_vals[4]; b_vals[0] = (b4 >> 0) & 0xFF; b_vals[1] = (b4 >> 8) & 0xFF; b_vals[2] = (b4 >> 16) & 0xFF; b_vals[3] = (b4 >> 24) & 0xFF; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++) { acc[i] += a * float(b_vals[i]) * __half2float(s[i]); } } #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++) { C[row * N + col_base + i] = __float2half_rn(acc[i]); } } ``` Wait, `(b4 >> 0) & 0xFF` etc. But `b4` is a `uint32_t`. When I shift a byte value into position and then AND with 0xFF, I get the byte. But when I assign to `int8_t`, I need to sign-extend properly. For example, if the byte is `0xF8` (as uint8_t, that's 248), converting to int8_t gives -8. This should work automatically in C++ if we use a signed type. Actually, let me be more careful: ```cpp uint32_t b4 = ...; int8_t b0 = static_cast<int8_t>(b4 & 0xFF); int8_t b1 = static_cast<int8_t>((b4 >> 8) & 0xFF); ... ``` This correctly sign-extends because `static_cast<int8_t>` from a value > 127 will give negative results. Wait, let me verify. If `b4 & 0xFF` is 248 (0xF8), then `static_cast<int8_t>(248)` is implementation-defined for values outside int8_t range in C++, but typically it wraps to -8. Actually no - converting an unsigned value to a signed type when it overflows is implementation-defined before C++20. In CUDA, for 2's complement, it typically wraps. But to be safe, I should use: ```cpp int8_t b0 = *reinterpret_cast<const int8_t*>(&b4); // But this would point to the first byte, not extract each byte. ``` Better: ```cpp uint32_t b4 = ...; int8_t b_vals[4]; b_vals[0] = (b4 << 24) >> 24; // sign extend low byte... wait, that doesn't work // Actually, for 8-bit sign extension: int32_t val = b4 & 0xFF; b_vals[0] = (val << 24) >> 24; // arithmetic shift right val = (b4 >> 8) & 0xFF; b_vals[1] = (val << 24) >> 24; val = (b4 >> 16) & 0xFF; b_vals[2] = (val << 24) >> 24; val = (b4 >> 24) & 0xFF; b_vals[3] = (val << 24) >> 24; ``` This properly sign-extends. But for performance, the compiler might already handle `static_cast<int8_t>` correctly. I'll use a helper: ```cpp __device__ inline int8_t extract_sign_byte(uint32_t val, int shift) { return static_cast<int8_t>((val >> shift) & 0xFF); } ``` In practice for CUDA, this compiles to a single `BFE` or shift+and instruction, and the cast is free (just reinterpreted). Now, let me think about `b4` loading. `B + k * N + col_base` must be aligned to 4 bytes for `uint32_t` load. `col_base = blockIdx.x * 128 + lane * 4`. blockIdx.x * 128 is divisible by 4. lane * 4 is divisible by 4. k * N where N=4096 is divisible by 4. And col_base * 2 is divisible by 8, so definitely divisible by 4, 8, etc. So half loads are also aligned. No, 256 is divisible by 8. lane * 8 is divisible by 8. So yes, the address is always divisible by 8. So I can load scales as `uint64_t` or `float2` or similar. Using `float2` for scales? Let me write a clean, well-structured first attempt. ```python import torch import torch.nn as nn from torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline CUDA_SOURCE = r''' #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> #include <stdint.h> #define N_PER_BLOCK 128 #define COLS_PER_THREAD 4 __global__ void gemm_w4a16_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const int8_t* __restrict__ B, const half* __restrict__ scales, half* __restrict__ C, int M, int N, int K, int group_size) { const int warp_id = threadIdx.x >> 5; Here is my code: ```python import torch import torch.nn as nn from torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline CUDA_SOURCE = r''' #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> #include <stdint.h> #define N_PER_BLOCK 128 #define COLS_PER_THREAD 4 #define BLOCK_THREADS 512 __device__ __forceinline__ int8_t extract_byte(uint32_t val, int idx) { return static_cast<int8_t>((val >> (idx * 8)) & 0xFF); } __global__ void gemm_w4a16_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const int8_t* __restrict__ B, const half* __restrict__ scales, half* __restrict__ C, int M, int N, int K, int group_size) { const int warp_id = threadIdx.x >> 5; i < COLS_PER_THREAD; ++i) { C[row * N + col_base + i] = __float2half_rn(acc[i]); } } torch::Tensor gemm_w4a16(torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor scales, int64_t group_size) { int M = A.size(0); return C; } PYBIND11_MODULE(TORCH_EXTENSION_NAME, m) { m.def("gemm_w4a16", &gemm_w4a16, "W4A16 groupwise GEMM for Hopper"); } ''' gemm_w4a16_mod = load_inline( name="gemm_w4a16_hopper", cpp_sources="", cuda_sources=CUDA_SOURCE, functions=["gemm_w4a16"], extra_cuda_cflags=[ "-O3", "--use_fast_math", "-gencode=arch=compute_90,code=sm_90", ], verbose=False, ) class ModelNew(nn.Module): def __init__(self, k: int, n: int, group_size: int): super().__init__() self.k = k self.n = n self.group_size = group_size w = torch.randn(k, n, dtype=torch.float16, device='cuda') num_groups = k // group_size w_grouped = w.view(num_groups, group_size, n) maxabs = w_grouped.abs().amax(dim=1, keepdim=True) scales = maxabs / 7.0 q = torch.round(w_grouped / scales).to(torch.int8) q = torch.clamp(q, -8, 7) self.register_buffer('qweight', q.view(k, n)) self.register_buffer('scales', scales.squeeze(1).contiguous()) def forward(self, a: torch.Tensor): return gemm_w4a16_mod.gemm_w4a16(a, self.qweight, self.scales, self.group_size) ``` I'm passing this to compile_kernel. i < COLS_PER_THREAD; i++) acc[i] = 0.0f;\n \n half scale_vals[COLS_PER_THREAD];\n int cur_g = -1;\n \n for (int k = 0; k < K; ++k) {\n int g = k / group_size;\n if (g != cur_g) {\n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0;
